New robust estimator for handling outliers and multicollinearity in gamma regression model with application to breast cancer data
Authors: Arwa M. Alshangiti, Ahmed M. Gemeay, Mahmoud E. Bakr, Abdirashid M. Yousuf, M. A. El-Qurashi, Oluwafemi Samson Balogun, Eslam Hussam, Ali T. Hammad
Published: 2025

ABSTRACT
The gamma regression model (GRM) is commonly used to analyze continuous data that are positively skewed. However, the GRM is sensitive to multicollinearity and outliers. These two problems often occur in regression analysis. Multicollinearity can make the estimated coefficients unstable and cause inflated variance, while outliers can lead to misleading results and reduce the reliability of statistical conclusions. To overcome these issues, we propose a new robust gamma ridge regression estimator that combines a ridge regression estimator with robust estimation methods such as M-estimation and Mallows-type estimators. The ridge part of the model helps to reduce the effects of multicollinearity, while the robust estimation minimizes the impact of outliers, leading to more accurate and stable results. We support our method with detailed theoretical comparisons and a series of Monte Carlo simulations under different conditions. The results show that our proposed model consistently performs better than the traditional gamma ridge regression estimator, especially when multicollinearity and outliers are present. Furthermore, we apply our model to a breast cancer dataset that contains both issues. The application confirms that our method provides more reliable coefficient estimates and better predictions compared to the conventional methods. Based on these results, we recommend using this robust gamma ridge regression estimator, especially in biomedical studies and other fields where multicollinearity and outliers are common problems.

Introduction

Gamma regression modeling (GRM) analyzes continuous, positive, right-skewed data characterized by greater variance relative to the mean. This is a kind of generalized linear model (GLM) that extends basic linear regression by addressing heteroscedasticity and non-normal distributions1–3. The model indicates that the response variable follows a gamma distribution, characterized by shape and scale parameters. It employs link functions, often inverse or logarithmic, to ensure that projected values remain positive4,5. GRM is very beneficial in domains such as healthcare (e.g., modeling hospital stay durations), economics (e.g., insurance claims), and engineering (e.g., time-to-failure analysis), where data often exhibit skewness and uneven variance6. GRM improves the prediction accuracy for favorable outcomes by using the inherent distributional characteristics of the data. In contrast, linear regression may provide unreasonably poor predictions because it assumes constant variance.

The GRM assumes, under the standard assumption, that there is no correlation among explanatory variables. Researchers may encounter situations where predictors have strong or almost strong linear relationships in actual contexts, leading to the multicollinearity issue first documented by Frisch7. Although maximum likelihood estimation (MLE) remains the standard approach for determining GRM coefficients, its efficacy may diminish considerably in the presence of multicollinearity. Multicollinear predictors tend to inflate the variance of MLEs, making statistical inference less reliable and often leading to instability in coefficient estimations. This challenge resembles the familiar issues in linear regression, where multicollinearity leads to exaggerated standard errors and diminished statistical power. Recognizing these issues, statisticians have developed many biased estimation strategies designed to intentionally introduce a little bias in exchange for considerable variance reduction. Numerous robust alternatives have been proposed throughout time, such as: the ridge regression estimator introduced by Hoerl and Kennard8,9, the Stein estimator defined by Brandwei and Strawderman10, the Liu estimator introduced by Kejian11, and others. Collectively, these strategies provide effective tools to mitigate the impact of multicollinearity and enhance the robustness of regression analyses.

Recently, many researchers have introduced some biased estimators to solve the multicollinearity problem in the GRM, such as: Algamal12 introduced a gamma ridge regression estimator, Lukman et al.13 developed a ridge-type estimator, Amin et al.14 defined some ridge estimators, Lukman et al.15 proposed a modified ridge-type estimator, Algamal16 presented shrinkage estimators, Seifollahi et al.17 suggested a restricted estimator as a solution to the multicollinearity, Lukman et al.18 proposed influence measures in the gamma modified ridge type estimator, and among others.

In real-world data, it’s common to find outliers, values that stand out and don’t follow the general trend of the data19,20. In GRM, which is often used when the outcome is positive and skewed, these outliers can have a big impact on how well the model estimates the parameters. Outliers can happen because of mistakes in measurement or because of rare but real events. Traditional methods like MLE struggle with outliers; they tend to produce estimates with large variances and biases, making the results less trustworthy. This problem is even more serious in GRM because the model already deals with skewed data, making it extra sensitive to outliers21–23. To handle this, researchers have developed robust estimation methods. Techniques like M-estimators, MM-estimators, and Mallows or Huber-type robust estimators help by reducing the effect of outliers on the results24–28. Using these robust methods in GRM leads to more stable models, gives better conclusions, and makes more accurate predictions, which is especially important in areas like healthcare and environmental research where outliers are often found.

As data science and analysis have grown, the data we work with has become more complicated and often contains problems like multicollinearity and outliers. Because of this, researchers have recently started focusing on developing robust estimators that can deal with both issues at the same time in GLM. They are combining biased estimators, which are good at handling multicollinearity, with robust estimators, which are designed to manage outliers. Some examples of this research include: Olaluwoye et al.29 defined a robust ridge estimator for the beta regression model, Arum et al.30 developed a robust modified jackknife ridge estimator for the Poisson regression model (PRM), Dawoud et al.31 presented a robust Kibria Lukman estimator for PRM, Lukman et al.32 suggested a robust ridge estimator for PRM, Lukman et al.33 introduced a robust Kibria–Lukman estimator for logistic regression model, Mohammad et al.34 developed a new robust two-parameter estimator for PRM, Alqasem et al.35 proposed a robust James-Stein estimator for PRM, Alghamdi et al.36 defined robust and non-robust modified Liu estimator for PRM, and among others. While there has been a lot of research on GRM, studies that focus on dealing with outliers are still very limited. In this paper, we introduce a new robust estimator that tackles both outliers and multicollinearity in GRM29,32,33. Our approach combines the ridge estimator with the Mallows quasi-likelihood estimator, offering a strong alternative to the traditional MLE and ridge estimator. To test how well our estimator performs, we provide theoretical comparisons and run Monte Carlo simulations. Both the theory and the simulation results show that our proposed estimator performs better when outliers and multicollinearity are present. To further prove its practical value, we apply our method to real-world data from breast cancer patients. The findings confirm that our estimator is more reliable and effective, based on both the theoretical analysis and simulation studies.

This paper is structured as follows: In Section 2, we review existing research on GRM and common estimation methods like MLE and ridge estimators, along with studies that address outliers in GRM. Section 3 introduces our proposed robust estimator, explaining its statistical properties, how it compares to existing methods, and how to choose its parameters. Section 4 describes the Monte Carlo simulation setup and discusses the results. In Section 5, we apply the proposed estimator to real data from breast cancer patients. Finally, Section 6 summarizes the main findings and conclusions of this study.

Methodological review

Gamma regression model

The GRM is useful when the response variable, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$y_i (i = 1, 2, \ldots , n)$$\end{document}, is positively skewed. In this model, the average is related to how spread out the data is, and the response follows a gamma distribution with two positive parameters, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\phi _1, \phi _2)$$\end{document}, which is written as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G(\phi _1, \phi _2)$$\end{document}. The probability density function for the gamma distribution is1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} f(y; \phi _1, \phi _2) = \frac{1}{\Gamma (\phi _1)\phi _2} y^{\phi _1 - 1} e^{-\frac{y}{\phi _2}}, \quad y > 0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Hardin and Hilbe37 explained how to apply GRM when the response follows a gamma distribution. They showed that the model can also be expressed as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G(\mu , \nu )$$\end{document}, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu = E(y)$$\end{document} represents the mean and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nu > 0$$\end{document} is the dispersion parameter. If we redefine the parameters as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phi _1 = \nu ^{-1}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phi _2 = \mu \nu$$\end{document}, the gamma density function becomes:2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} f(y; \mu , \nu ) = \frac{1}{\Gamma (\nu ^{-1})} \left( \frac{1}{\mu \nu } \right) ^{\nu ^{-1}} y^{\nu ^{-1} - 1} e^{-\frac{y}{\mu \nu }}, \quad y > 0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}The gamma distribution is part of the GLMs, which have a probability density function in the exponential family form:3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} f(y; \Theta , \nu ) = \exp \left[ \frac{y\Theta - b(\Theta )}{a(\nu )} + c(y, \nu ) \right] , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Theta$$\end{document} is the location parameter, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nu$$\end{document} is the dispersion parameter, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b(\Theta )$$\end{document} is the cumulant function. If we set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Theta = \frac{1}{\mu }$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a(\nu ) = \nu$$\end{document}, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b(\Theta ) = -\ln (\mu )$$\end{document}, the gamma density function can be written as:4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} f(y; \mu , \nu ) = \exp \left[ \frac{\frac{y}{\mu }- \left( -\ln (\mu ) \right) }{-\nu } + \frac{1 - \nu }{\nu } \ln (y) - \frac{\ln (\nu )}{\nu } - \ln \Gamma \left( \frac{1}{\nu } \right) \right] . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}In GRM, the most commonly used link function is the reciprocal function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g(\mu _i) = \frac{1}{\mu _i} = x_i^T \beta ,$$\end{document} where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_i$$\end{document} is the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i$$\end{document}-th row of the data matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X$$\end{document}, which has \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n$$\end{document} rows and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r$$\end{document} columns for the explanatory variables. The vector \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta$$\end{document} holds the unknown regression coefficients, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g(\cdot )$$\end{document} represents the link function. The log-likelihood for this GRM can then be written as:5\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \ell = \log l(y; \mu , \nu ) = \sum _{i=1}^{n} \left[ \frac{\frac{y_i}{\mu _i} - \left( -\ln (\mu _i) \right) }{-\nu }+ \frac{1 - \nu }{\nu } \ln (y_i) - \frac{\ln (\nu )}{\nu } - \ln \Gamma \left( \frac{1}{\nu } \right) \right] . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}The estimates \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\hat{\beta }}$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\hat{\mu }}$$\end{document}, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\hat{\nu }}$$\end{document} are found using the MLE. This is done by maximizing the log-likelihood function in Equation (5) using the Newton-Raphson iterative method. To find these estimates, we solve the following equation:6\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} S(\beta ) = \frac{\partial \ell }{\partial \beta } = \frac{\partial \ell }{\partial \phi _i} \frac{\partial \phi _i}{\partial \beta } = \frac{1}{\nu } \sum _{i=1}^n \left( y_i - \frac{1}{x_i^T \beta } \right) x_i = 0, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S(\beta )$$\end{document} is called the score vector. Since Equation (6) is a nonlinear equation, we use an iterative method called Newton-Raphson to estimate the unknown parameters. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta ^{(r)}$$\end{document} be the estimate for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta$$\end{document} after \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r$$\end{document} iterations. The next estimate is given by:7\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \beta ^{(r+1)} = \beta ^{(r)} + \left\{ I \left( \beta ^{(r)} \right) \right\} ^{-1} S \left( \beta ^{(r)} \right) , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I \left( \beta ^{(r)} \right) = \left\{ -\text {E} \left( \frac{\partial ^2 L}{\partial \beta \partial \beta ^T} \right) \right\}$$\end{document} is the Fisher information matrix. Both \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S(\beta ^{(r)})$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I(\beta ^{(r)})$$\end{document} are calculated based on the current estimate \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta ^{(r)}$$\end{document}. Once the process converges, we can find the gamma maximum likelihood estimator (GMLE) given by:8\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\hat{\beta }}_{\text {GMLE}} = \left( X^T {\hat{V}} X \right) ^{-1} X^T {\hat{V}} c^*, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\hat{V}} = \text {diag} \left\{ {\hat{\mu }}_1^2, {\hat{\mu }}_2^2, \ldots , {\hat{\mu }}_n^2 \right\}$$\end{document}, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c^* = x_i^T {\hat{\beta }} + \frac{y_i - {\hat{\mu }}_i}{{\hat{\mu }}_i^2}$$\end{document} is the adjusted response variable. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\hat{\mu }}_i = \frac{1}{x_i^T\beta }$$\end{document} is the estimated mean calculated with the inverse link function. Both \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\hat{V}}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c^*$$\end{document} are updated in the iterative Fisher scoring process. The matrix mean squared error (MMSE) for GMLE (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\hat{\beta }}_{\text {GMLE}}$$\end{document}) is:9\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \text {MMSE} \left( {\hat{\beta }}_{\text {GMLE}} \right) = {\hat{\nu }} \left( X^T {\hat{V}} X \right) ^{-1}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}To find the mean squared error (MSE) of the GMLE, we take the trace of the MMSE:10\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \text {MSE} \left( {\hat{\beta }}_{\text {GMLE}} \right) = E \left( {\hat{\beta }}_{\text {GMLE}} - \beta \right) ^T \left( {\hat{\beta }}_{\text {GMLE}} - \beta \right) = \text {tr} \left( \text {MMSE} \left( {\hat{\beta }}_{\text {GMLE}} \right) \right) = {\hat{\nu }} \sum _{j=1}^r \frac{1}{\lambda _j}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\hat{\nu }} = (n - r)^{-1} \sum _{i=1}^n \frac{(y_i - {\hat{\mu }}_i)^2}{({\hat{\mu }}_i)^2}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _j$$\end{document} is the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$j$$\end{document}-th eigenvalue of the matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X^T {\hat{V}} X$$\end{document}. The MSE of the GMLE becomes higher when the explanatory variables are highly correlated, as some eigenvalues may be small, making the matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X^T {\hat{V}} X$$\end{document} ill-conditioned.

Gamma ridge regression estimator

Based on the work of Segerstedt38, who generalized the work of Hoerl and Kennard8 and applied it to GLMs, many researchers have introduced ridge estimators for various GLMs. These ridge estimators are effective in handling multicollinearity by adding a small value, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k$$\end{document}, to the diagonal elements of the variance-covariance matrix, which significantly reduces multicollinearity. For example, Månsson and Shukur39, Schaefer et al.40, Rady et al.41, Månsson42, Tharshan et al.43, Sami et al.44, and Shahzad et al.45.

Following the previous works, Algamal12 introduced a ridge regression estimator for GRM to handle multicollinearity. The equation for the gamma ridge regression estimator (GRRE) is:11\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\hat{\beta }}_{\text {GRRE}} = \left( X^T {\hat{V}} X + kI_r \right) ^{-1} X^T {\hat{V}} X {\hat{\beta }}_{\text {GMLE}}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k$$\end{document} is the ridge parameter (with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k > 0$$\end{document}), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I_r$$\end{document} is the identity matrix, and when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k = 0$$\end{document}, the GRRE simplifies to the GMLE. The bias vector and the variance-covariance matrix of the GRRE are:12\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \text {Bias}\left( {\hat{\beta }}_{\text {GRRE}} \right) = \text {E}\left( {\hat{\beta }}_{\text {GRRE}} \right) - \beta = -k P (\Lambda +kI_r)^{-1} \alpha _{MLE}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}and13\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \text {Cov}\left( {\hat{\beta }}_{\text {GRRE}} \right) = {\hat{\nu }}~ P(\Lambda +kI_r)^{-1} \Lambda (\Lambda +kI_r)^{-1} P^T. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}The MSE and MMSE for the GRRE can be calculated using the bias and covariance formulas:14\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\text {MMSE}\left( {\hat{\beta }}_{\text {GRRE}} \right) ={\hat{\nu }}~ P(\Lambda +kI_r)^{-1} \Lambda (\Lambda +kI_r)^{-1} P^T + {k}^2 P (\Lambda +kI_r)^{-1} \alpha _{MLE} \alpha _{MLE}^T (\Lambda +kI_r)^{-1} P^T, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}15\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\text {MSE}\left( {\hat{\beta }}_{\text {GRRE}} \right) = \text {tr} \left[ \text {MMSE}\left( {\hat{\beta }}_{\text {GRRE}} \right) \right] = {\hat{\nu }} \sum ^{r}_{j=1}\frac{\lambda _j}{(\lambda _j + k )^{2}} + {k}^{2} \sum ^{r}_{j=1} \frac{\alpha _{MLE_j}^2}{(\lambda _j + k )^{2}}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Here, the matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X^T {\hat{V}} X$$\end{document} is expressed as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X^T {\hat{V}} X = P \Lambda P^T$$\end{document}, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$P$$\end{document} is an orthogonal matrix whose columns are the eigenvectors of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X^T {\hat{V}} X$$\end{document}. The vector \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha _{MLE}$$\end{document} is defined as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$P^{T} {\hat{\beta }}_{\text {GMLE}}$$\end{document}, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Lambda$$\end{document} is a diagonal matrix with eigenvalues of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X^T {\hat{W}} X$$\end{document}, represented as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Lambda = \text {diag}(\lambda _1, \lambda _2, \dots , \lambda _{p})$$\end{document}.

Gamma robust estimators

The GMLE and GRRE can be sensitive to outliers, which makes it necessary to find more robust alternatives in the GRM. As a result, the robust estimator has been developed to handle outliers in this context. This section gives an overview of the existing robust estimator for the GRM.

In the framework of a GRM with a link function, the random response variable \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$y_i$$\end{document} for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i = 1, \ldots , n$$\end{document} follows these properties:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} E\left[ Y_i|{x}_i\right] = \mu _i, \quad V\left[ Y_i|{x}_i\right] = v(\mu _i), \quad g(\mu _i) = {x}_i^{T} \beta , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta \in {\mathbb {R}}^p$$\end{document} is the parameter vector, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${x}_i \in {\mathbb {R}}^r$$\end{document} is the set of explanatory variables, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g(\cdot )$$\end{document} is the link function, and the variance \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v(\mu _i)$$\end{document} is proportional to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _i^2$$\end{document}. This type of model works well for positively skewed data, such as cost data28,46. It uses a multiplicative form:16\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} y_i = g(\mu _i) \varepsilon _i, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where the error term \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon _i$$\end{document} has a constant variance.

Cantoni and Ronchetti27,28 proposed a robust estimator for the GRM, based on a system of M-estimating equations for the regression parameters \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta$$\end{document}47. These equations are written as follows:17\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \sum \limits _{i=1}^{n} \psi \left( y_i, \beta , v\right) = 0, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\psi$$\end{document} is a function based on Huber’s method or Mallows, applied to the Pearson residuals to reduce the influence of outliers, then the Mallows quasi-likelihood estimator of GRM is given by:18\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \sum \limits _{i=1}^{n} \left[ \psi (r_i) w({x}_i) \frac{1}{v^{1/2}(\mu _i)} \mu _i' - a(\beta ) \right] = 0, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ r_i = (y_i - \mu _i)/v^{1/2}(\mu _i) $$\end{document} as the Pearson residuals, et of weights \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w({x}_i)$$\end{document} to down-weight leverage points, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \mu _i' = \partial \mu _i/\partial \beta $$\end{document}.

The term19\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} a(\beta ) = \frac{1}{n} \sum \limits _{i=1}^{n} \text {E}\left[ \psi (r_i) w({x}_i) \frac{1}{v^{1/2}(\mu _i)} \mu _i' \right] \end{aligned}$$\end{document}is included to ensure that the estimator is consistent with the mean parameter \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu$$\end{document} in the model. To estimate \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v$$\end{document}, Huber’s Proposal 2 method is used26.

Proposed Robust Estimator

Robust gamma ridge regression estimator

Building on the work of Olaluwoye et al.29, Lukman et al.32, and Lukman et al.33. This paper derives a proposed robust estimator for the GRM by combining the ridge regression estimator with the robust Mallows quasi-likelihood estimator. The resulting hybrid approaches are called robust gamma ridge regression (RGRRE-M) estimators, which can handle outliers and multicollinearity. The following equation gives the expression for the RGRRE-M for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta$$\end{document}:20\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\hat{\beta }}_{\text {RGRRE-M}} = \left( X^T {\hat{V}} X + k_0I_r \right) ^{-1} X^T {\hat{V}} X {\hat{\beta }}_{\text {GMQLE}}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\hat{\beta }}_{\text {GMQLE}}=\left( X^T {\hat{V}}^* X \right) ^{-1} X^T {\hat{V}}^* c^*$$\end{document} with the weighted matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\hat{V}}^*=\text {diag}\{a_1(\beta ),\dots ,a_n(\beta )\}$$\end{document}, denotes the robust gamma Mallows quasi-likelihood estimator, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k_0$$\end{document} is the RGRRE-M parameter (with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k_0 > 0$$\end{document}) and when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k = 0$$\end{document}, and the RGRRE-M simplifies to the GMQLE. The bias vector and the variance-covariance matrix of the RGRRE-M are:21\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \text {Bias}\left( {\hat{\beta }}_{\text {RGRRE-M}} \right)&= \text {E}\left( {\hat{\beta }}_{\text {RGRRE-M}} \right) - \beta \\ &= \left( X^T {\hat{V}} X + k_0I_r \right) ^{-1} X^T {\hat{V}} X ~~\text {E}\left( {\hat{\beta }}_{\text {GMQLE}} \right) =-k P (\Lambda +k_0I_r)^{-1} \alpha _{MQLE}, \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}and22\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \text {Cov}\left( {\hat{\beta }}_{\text {RGRRE-M}} \right)&=\text {E}\Bigg [\left( {\hat{\beta }}_{\text {RGRRE-M}}-\text {E}({\hat{\beta }}_{\text {RGRRE-M}}) \right) \left( {\hat{\beta }}_{\text {RGRRE-M}}-\text {E}({\hat{\beta }}_{\text {RGRRE-M}}) \right) ^T\Bigg ] \\ &=\left( X^T {\hat{V}} X + k_0I_r \right) ^{-1} X^T {\hat{V}} X ~~\text {Cov}\left( {\hat{\beta }}_{\text {GMQLE}} \right) X^T {\hat{V}} X\left( X^T {\hat{V}} X + k_0I_r \right) ^{-1} \\ &={\hat{\nu }}~ P(\Lambda +k_0I_r)^{-1} \Lambda \Omega \Lambda (\Lambda +k_0I_r)^{-1} P^T. \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}The MSE and MMSE for the RGRRE-M can be calculated using the bias and covariance formulas:23\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\begin{aligned} \text {MMSE}\left( {\hat{\beta }}_{\text {RGRRE-M}} \right)&= \text {Cov}\left( {\hat{\beta }}_{\text {RGRRE-M}} \right) +\text {Bais}\left( {\hat{\beta }}_{\text {RGRRE-M}} \right) {\textbf {}}{Bais}^{T}\left( {\hat{\beta }}_{\text {RGRRE-M}} \right) \\ &={\hat{\nu }}~ P(\Lambda +k_0I_r)^{-1} \Lambda \Omega \Lambda (\Lambda +k_0I_r)^{-1} P^T+k_0^2 P (\Lambda +k_0I_r)^{-1}\\ &\times \alpha _{MQLE}\alpha _{MQLE}^T (\Lambda +k_0I_r)^{-1}P^T, \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}24\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\text {MSE}\left( {\hat{\beta }}_{\text {RGRRE-M}} \right) = \text {tr} \left[ \text {MMSE}\left( {\hat{\beta }}_{\text {RGRRE-M}} \right) \right] = {\hat{\nu }} \sum ^{r}_{j=1}\frac{\lambda _j^2\omega _{jj}}{(\lambda _j + k_0)^{2}} + {k}_0^{2} \sum ^{r}_{j=1} \frac{\alpha _{MQLE_j}^2}{(\lambda _j + k_0 )^{2}}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha _{MQLE}=P^T{\hat{\beta }}_{GMQLE}$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {MMSE}({\hat{\beta }}_{\text {MQLE}}) = \Omega , \quad \text {and} \quad \text {MSE}({\hat{\beta }}_{\text {MQLE}}) = \sum _{j=1}^{r} \omega _{jj}.$$\end{document}

Theoretical comparisons between estimators

Lemma 1

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta$$\end{document} be a positive definite matrix, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c$$\end{document} a nonzero constant vector, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a$$\end{document} a positive integer. The matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a\eta - cc'$$\end{document} will be positive definite if and only if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c'\eta c < a$$\end{document}. Refer to Farebrother, 197648.

Lemma 2

Estimate the parameter \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\zeta$$\end{document} using two estimators, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\hat{\zeta }}_1$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\hat{\zeta }}_2$$\end{document}. According to the MMSE, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta ({\hat{\zeta }}_1, {\hat{\zeta }}_2) = \text {MMSE}({\hat{\zeta }}_1) - \text {MMSE}({\hat{\zeta }}_2)$$\end{document}, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\hat{\zeta }}_1$$\end{document} outperforms \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\hat{\zeta }}_2$$\end{document} when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta < 0$$\end{document}. This only signifies that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\hat{\zeta }}_1$$\end{document} has a lower MMSE than \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\hat{\zeta }}_2$$\end{document}.

Lemma 3

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$y$$\end{document} represent the observed data, and define the two estimators \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\hat{\zeta }}_1$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\hat{\zeta }}_2$$\end{document} as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\hat{\zeta }}_1 = Z_1 y$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\hat{\zeta }}_2 = Z_2 y$$\end{document}, respectively. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {Var-Cov}({\hat{\zeta }}_1)$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {Var-Cov}({\hat{\zeta }}_2)$$\end{document} possess their corresponding variance-covariance matrices. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi$$\end{document} be defined as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {Var-Cov}({\hat{\zeta }}_1) - \operatorname {Var-Cov}({\hat{\zeta }}_2).$$\end{document} If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi$$\end{document} is positive definite, the difference in their MMSEs may be articulated as:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Delta ({\hat{\zeta }}_1, {\hat{\zeta }}_2) = \Psi + z_1 z_1' - z_2 z_2' \end{aligned}$$\end{document}. Refer to Trenkler49. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z_1$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z_2$$\end{document} are the bias vectors corresponding to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\hat{\zeta }}_1$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\hat{\zeta }}_2$$\end{document}, respectively.

Furthermore, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta ({\hat{\zeta }}_1, {\hat{\zeta }}_2) > 0$$\end{document} if and only if:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} z_2'(\Psi + z_1 z_1')z_2 < 1. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}

Theorem 1

If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sum _{j=1}^{r}\omega _{jj} < \sum _{j=1}^{r}\frac{1}{\lambda _j}$$\end{document}, the estimator \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\hat{\beta }}_{\text {RGRRE-M}}$$\end{document} is outperform to the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\hat{\beta }}_{\text {GMLE}}$$\end{document} under the MMSE criterion iff:25\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \text {Bias}({\hat{\beta }}_{\text {RGRRE-M}})^{T}[\Lambda ^{-1} - (\Lambda +k_0I_r)^{-1} \Lambda \Omega \Lambda (\Lambda +k_0I_r)^{-1}]\text {Bias}({\hat{\beta }}_{\text {RGRRE-M}}) < 1. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}

Proof

The difference is expressed in the covariance of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\hat{\beta }}_{\text {GMLE}}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\hat{\beta }}_{\text {RGRRE-M}}$$\end{document} is:26\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \text {Cov}({\hat{\beta }}_{\text {GMLE}}) - \text {Cov}({\hat{\beta }}_{\text {RGRRE-M}}) = \Lambda ^{-1} - (\Lambda +k_0I_r)^{-1} \Lambda \Omega \Lambda (\Lambda +k_0I_r)^{-1}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}This difference can be expressed as:27\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \text {Cov}({\hat{\beta }}_{\text {GMLE}}) - \text {Cov}({\hat{\beta }}_{\text {RGRRE-M}}) = \sum _{j=1}^{r}\frac{1}{\lambda _{j}} - \sum _{j=1}^{r} \frac{{\lambda }_j^2\omega _{jj}}{({\lambda }_j+k_0)^2}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Given that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {Bias}({\hat{\beta }}_{\text {RGRRE-M}})\text {Bias}({\hat{\beta }}_{\text {RGRRE-M}})^{T}$$\end{document} is nonnegative definite, the difference matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Lambda ^{-1} - (\Lambda +k_0I_r)^{-1} \Lambda \Omega \Lambda (\Lambda +k_0I_r)^{-1}$$\end{document} is positive definite iff \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sum _{j=1}^{r}\frac{1}{\lambda _{j}} - \sum _{j=1}^{r} \frac{{\lambda }_j^2\omega _{jj}}{({\lambda }_j+k_0)^2} > 0$$\end{document} for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k_0 > 0$$\end{document}. This indicates that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Lambda ^{-1} - (\Lambda +k_0I_r)^{-1} \Lambda \Omega \Lambda (\Lambda +k_0I_r)^{-1}$$\end{document} is positive definite, therefore affirming that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\hat{\beta }}_{\text {RGRRE-M}}$$\end{document} has a reduced covariance matrix, making it superior to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\hat{\beta }}_{\text {GMLE}}$$\end{document}. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square$$\end{document}

Theorem 2

If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sum _{j=1}^{r}\omega _{jj} < \sum _{j=1}^{r}\frac{1}{\lambda _j}$$\end{document}, the estimator \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\hat{\beta }}_{\text {RGRRE-M}}$$\end{document} is outperform to the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\hat{\beta }}_{\text {GRRE}}$$\end{document} under the MMSE criterion iff:28\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \text {Bias}({\hat{\beta }}_{\text {RGRRE-M}})^{T}[(\Lambda +kI_r)^{-1} \Lambda (\Lambda +kI_r)^{-1} - (\Lambda +k_0I_r)^{-1} \Lambda \Omega \Lambda (\Lambda +k_0I_r)^{-1}]\text {Bias}({\hat{\beta }}_{\text {RGRRE-M}}) < 1. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}

Proof

The difference is expressed in the covariance of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\hat{\beta }}_{\text {GRRE}}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\hat{\beta }}_{\text {RGRRE-M}}$$\end{document} is:29\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \text {Cov}({\hat{\beta }}_{\text {GRRE}}) - \text {Cov}({\hat{\beta }}_{\text {RGRRE-M}}) =(\Lambda +kI_r)^{-1} \Lambda (\Lambda +kI_r)^{-1} - (\Lambda +k_0I_r)^{-1} \Lambda \Omega \Lambda (\Lambda +k_0I_r)^{-1}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}This difference can be expressed as:30\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \text {Cov}({\hat{\beta }}_{\text {GRRE}}) - \text {Cov}({\hat{\beta }}_{\text {RGRRE-M}}) = \sum _{j=1}^{r}\frac{\lambda _{j}}{(\lambda _{j}+k)^2} - \sum _{j=1}^{r} \frac{{\lambda }_j^2\omega _{jj}}{({\lambda }_j+k_0)^2}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Given that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {Bias}({\hat{\beta }}_{\text {RGRRE-M}})\text {Bias}({\hat{\beta }}_{\text {RGRRE-M}})^{T}$$\end{document} is nonnegative definite, the difference matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\Lambda +kI_r)^{-1} \Lambda (\Lambda +kI_r)^{-1} - (\Lambda +k_0I_r)^{-1} \Lambda \Omega \Lambda (\Lambda +k_0I_r)^{-1}$$\end{document} is positive definite iff \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sum _{j=1}^{r}\frac{\lambda _{j}}{(\lambda _{j}+k)^2} - \sum _{j=1}^{r} \frac{{\lambda }_j^2\omega _{jj}}{({\lambda }_j+k_0)^2} > 0$$\end{document} for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k_0,~k > 0$$\end{document}. This indicates that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\Lambda +kI_r)^{-1} \Lambda (\Lambda +kI_r)^{-1} - (\Lambda +k_0I_r)^{-1} \Lambda \Omega \Lambda (\Lambda +k_0I_r)^{-1}$$\end{document} is positive definite, therefore affirming that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\hat{\beta }}_{\text {RGRRE-M}}$$\end{document} has a reduced covariance matrix, making it superior to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\hat{\beta }}_{\text {GRRE}}$$\end{document}. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square$$\end{document}

Selection of biasing parameters

The efficacy of the RGRRE-M is significantly determined by the judicious selection of the biasing parameter \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k$$\end{document}. These parameters regulate the balance between bias and variance in the estimating process, guaranteeing that the estimator attains optimum performance.

Following the work of Algamal12,16 and Amin et al.14, who nominated the following k values as the best choice for the GRRE.31\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&{\hat{k}}^1=\frac{{\hat{\nu }}}{\sum _{j=1}^r{\hat{\alpha }}_{MLE_j}^2}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}32\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&{\hat{k}}^2=\min \left( \frac{{\hat{\nu }}}{{\hat{\alpha }}_{MLE_j}^2}\right) . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}33\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&{\hat{k}}^3=\frac{{\hat{\nu }}}{r\sum _{j=1}^r{\hat{\alpha }}_{MLE_j}^2}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}34\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&{\hat{k}}^4=\prod _{j=1}^r\left( \frac{1}{\sqrt{\frac{{\hat{\nu }}}{{\hat{\alpha }}_{MLE_j}^2}}}\right) ^{\frac{1}{r}}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}35\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&{\hat{k}}^5=\min \left( \frac{{\hat{\nu }}\lambda _j}{\lambda _j{\hat{\alpha }}_{MLE_j}+{\hat{\nu }}}\right) . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}For RGRRE-M, we will use the same values of k in GRRE as before, but replace \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\hat{\alpha }}_{MLE_j}$$\end{document} by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\hat{\alpha }}_{MQLE_j}$$\end{document}36\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&{\hat{k}}_0^1=\frac{{\hat{\nu }}}{\sum _{j=1}^r{\hat{\alpha }}_{MQLE_j}^2}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}37\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&{\hat{k}}_0^2=\min \left( \frac{{\hat{\nu }}}{{\hat{\alpha }}_{MQLE_j}^2}\right) . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}38\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&{\hat{k}}_0^3=\frac{{\hat{\nu }}}{r\sum _{j=1}^r{\hat{\alpha }}_{MQLE_j}^2}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}39\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&{\hat{k}}_0^4=\prod _{j=1}^r\left( \frac{1}{\sqrt{\frac{{\hat{\nu }}}{{\hat{\alpha }}_{MQLE_j}^2}}}\right) ^{\frac{1}{r}}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}40\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&{\hat{k}}_0^5=\min \left( \frac{{\hat{\nu }}\lambda _j}{\lambda _j{\hat{\alpha }}_{MQLE_j}+{\hat{\nu }}}\right) . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}

Monte Carlo Simulation Study

This section outlines the simulation experiment devised to assess the efficacy of the proposed RGRRE-M estimator in addressing outliers and multicollinearity within the framework of a GRM. Initially, we generated the dependent variable \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$y_i$$\end{document} for the GRM using a reciprocal link function. The dependent variable adheres to a Gamma distribution with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$y_i \sim \text {Gamma} (\mu _i, \nu ) ; \quad i = 1, \ldots , n$$\end{document}. In this context, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _i$$\end{document} represents the mean, defined as50:41\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mu _{i} = E(y_{i}) = \left( \beta _{1}x_{i1} + \beta _{2}x_{i2} + \ldots + \beta _{r}x_{ir}\right) ^{-1}, \quad i = 1,2,\ldots ,n, \quad j = 1,2,\ldots ,r. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Subsequently, we established multicollinearity among the explanatory factors. The \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_i$$\end{document} values were produced using the formula29,31,51:42\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} x_{ij} = \left( 1 - \rho ^{2}\right) ^{1/2}z_{ij} + \rho z_{i(j+1)}, \quad i = 1,2,\ldots ,n, \quad j = 1,2,\ldots ,r \end{aligned}$$\end{document}In this formula, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho$$\end{document} denotes the correlation among explanatory variables, assuming values such as 0.85, 0.90, 0.95, 0.99, and 0.999. The \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z_{ij}$$\end{document}’s are independent standard normal pseudorandom variables. We identified the actual values of the regression parameters, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta$$\end{document}, by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta ^T \beta = 1$$\end{document}, as detailed in22,50. We evaluated several sample sizes: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n = 30, 75, 100, 200, 300, 400$$\end{document}, and established the dispersion values \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nu = 0.33, 0.67, 2$$\end{document}.

To evaluate the estimator’s resilience against outliers, we introduced a solitary outlier to the dependent variable by substituting one observation with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$y_i = y_i + a_0$$\end{document}, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_0 = \overline{y_i} + 3(V(y_i))$$\end{document}. We constructed many outliers in the dependent variable, constituting 10% and 20% of the data, with each outlier produced similarly22.

We assessed the estimator’s performance using the MSE, determined as the average squared deviation between the estimated and actual parameter values after 5000 simulated replications. All simulations were conducted in R, using the glm function for standard estimation.
